&lt;project_context>
# 项目: simgate-modular-routing

## 运行环境
- 服务器名: westb-13313
- SSH 账户: root（调用 ssh_execute / submit_training_job 时传入 account="root"）
- SSH 连接命令: ssh connect.westb.seetacloud.com  # 必须用此命令，~/.ssh/config 自动应用密钥和跳板机，禁止用裸 IP
- 项目代码根目录: /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing


## 环境搭建
连接服务器后先调 `detect_image` 检测是否为预置镜像。如果匹配，跳过基础环境搭建，只装项目特有依赖。已预装的包不要重复安装。

## 输出风格
回复简洁直接。只输出执行结果和必要信息，不要解释过程、不要客套、不要给未被要求的建议。
&lt;/project_context>
## 任务：R11 修复后最终同步 + 编译验证

### 步骤

1. **读取本地文件**（重点：references.bib + 可能改动的 .tex 文件）：
   用 `read_project_file` 读取：
   - `docs/paper/references.bib`
   - `docs/paper/main.tex`
   - `docs/paper/experiments.tex`
   - `docs/paper/method.tex`
   - `docs/paper/introduction.tex`
   - `docs/paper/appendix.tex`（如果存在）

2. **传输到远程**：
   SSH 到 westb-13313，对每个有变化的文件用 heredoc 写入：
   ```bash
   cat > /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/&lt;filename> << 'ENDOFFILE'
   &lt;content>
   ENDOFFILE
   ```
   单引号包裹分隔符防止 shell 展开 LaTeX 特殊字符。

3. **编译**：
   ```bash
   cd /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper
   pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode main.tex
   bibtex main
   pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode main.tex
   pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode main.tex
   ```

4. **验证**：
   ```bash
   # 确认 Cameron 2008 正常渲染
   grep -i "cameron" main.bbl && echo "OK: Cameron found in bbl" || echo "WARNING: Cameron NOT in bbl"
   
   # undefined citations
   grep "Citation.*undefined" main.log || echo "0 undefined citations"
   
   # errors
   grep "^!" main.log || echo "0 errors"
   
   # pages
   pdfinfo main.pdf | grep Pages
   
   # file size
   ls -la main.pdf
   ```

5. **汇报**：errors, undefined citations, Cameron 2008 渲染状态, 页数

### 环境
- 服务器: westb-13313
- 远程路径: `/root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/`
- Python: `source /root/miniconda3/bin/activate`
- 编译: pdflatex + bibtex

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
10	But this assumption has never been systematically verified: \emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}
11	
12	We propose a \emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals a surprisingly concentrated answer: \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95, penalty-independent); under ScienceWorld's default penalty ($P{=}100$), this corresponds to DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$).
13	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
14	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$.
15	
16	This framework addresses a methodological gap.
17	The standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.
18	In ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, leaving no step-level signal to learn from (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
19	Keyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\S\ref{sec:baselines}).
20	Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\S\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.
21	
22	Our prescreening selected task types showing \emph{any} modality difference (not death-specific); the finding that 19 of 30 task types show no routing signal itself confirms signal sparsity. Among the 11 selected types (245 episode pairs), the full deterministic advantage is $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$ points.
23	Excluding death episodes, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 78.9\% of non-tie labels (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry, a penalty-independent measure.
24	The penalty-sensitive Death Avoidance Factor (DAF; \S\ref{sec:daf}) is $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$) at the default penalty $P{=}100$, ranging from 50\% ($P{=}10$) to 98\% ($P{=}500$).
25	The per-task correlation between death-rate differential and performance gap is $r = 0.97$ ($p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$). Since $P$ is constant across task types, $\delta \cdot P$ is perfectly collinear with $\delta$; this near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance rather than an independent empirical relationship (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
26	
27	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
28	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
29	Without death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.
30	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.
31	An exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	Our contributions are:
34	\begin{enumerate}
35	    \item \textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
36	
37	    \item \textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
38	
39	    \item \textbf{Framework flexibility illustration} across environments (MATH, $N{=}795$: confirmed null, $p = 0.81$, no routing signal without death; ALFWorld, $N{=}135$: information asymmetry mechanism identified) and models (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot: directional consistency confirmed). These demonstrate diagnostic applicability, not universality of the ScienceWorld finding; true quantitative replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
40	\end{enumerate}
41	
42	
43
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
4	\label{sec:death-decomp}
5	
6	\begin{figure}[t]
7	\centering
8	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
9	\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\ performance gap ($\Delta_{\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}
10	\label{fig:decomposition}
11	\end{figure}
12	
13	Across 245 episodes, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$; at default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Of these 75, 54 (72.0\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely (both modalities score zero without death); death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the sole discriminating signal between modalities. \paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
14	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$). Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = P \cdot \delta + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ makes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\delta$ with residual $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \leq 25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Signal concentration.}
17	The SCI (\S\ref{sec:sci}) over per-task routing signal contributions is $0.2147$ (low concentration): the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths (DAF $= 0\%$, $\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores); all 10 remaining types exhibit DAF $= 100\%$. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.
18	
19	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}
20	Episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\% CI of $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for the aggregate DAF, excluding both 50\% and 100\%. This CI quantifies within-sample uncertainty; generalization to unseen task types requires held-out evaluation. Per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size. As a sensitivity analysis accounting for within-task-type correlation, cluster bootstrap resampling ($K{=}11$ task-type clusters, $B = 10{,}000$) yields a wider 95\% CI $[73.3\%, 100\%]$. With $K{=}11$ clusters, this interval should be interpreted cautiously: \citet{cameron-2008} recommend $K \geq 40$ for reliable cluster bootstrap inference. The lower bound nonetheless remains well above 50\%.
21	
22	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
23	\label{sec:cross-model}
24	
25	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\%$ vs.\ deterministic $0\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; 70B-class models and Reflexion-augmented reasoning represent testable predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
26	
27	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
28	\label{sec:math-contrast}
29	
30	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The McNemar test compares binary correctness: 80 problems are solved only by internal reasoning and 76 only by code, yielding near-symmetric discordance ($\chi^2 = 0.10$). The label distribution is balanced: 107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, with 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}. The confirmed null rules out any routing signal of magnitude ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. The absence of systematic routing signal confirms that without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage in this domain.
31	
32	\subsection{Baselines}
33	\label{sec:baselines}
34	
35	\begin{table}[t]
36	\centering
37	\small
38	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
39	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
40	\toprule
41	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
42	\midrule
43	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
44	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
45	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
46	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
47	\midrule
48	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}}
51	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single seed; see \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}.}
52	\label{tab:baselines}
53	\end{table}
54	
55	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
56	
57	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
58	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
59	
60	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \pm 3.5\%$, $58.2 \pm 4.0\%$, $59.2 \pm 3.5\%$; all within seed variance), indicating step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \pm 2.6\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \pm 1.0\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\%$); this non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales.
61	
62	\paragraph{Data scaling and deployment.} Augmentation-based data scaling ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}), but paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class (89.6\%$\rightarrow$97.7\%) without generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling (requiring ${\sim}1{,}350$ episode pairs for 50 minority-class examples) remains an open question. Capacity scaling improves episode-level routing ($+9$\,pp at 4B) without improving step-level accuracy (flat ${\sim}57\%$); the extractable signal is task-type-level structure. The cost of this extraction (4B for $+9$\,pp) is consistent with our analytical methodology being more efficient than brute-force learned routing. Deployed end-to-end (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), the router ($-10.70$) does not outperform always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). Label quality is verified in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.
63	
64	Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.
65	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\S\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
7	\label{sec:problem-setup}
8	
9	Consider an interactive environment $\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \pi(o_1, \ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.
10	
11	We define a modality set $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Internal} ($m = {\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.
14	    \item \textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.
15	\end{itemize}
16	
17	\noindent We restrict $\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning (deep internal) against direct-answer generation (fast internal) on 6 overlapping task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition) found no significant difference on any task (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes in this setting.
18	
19	A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.
20	
21	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
22	\label{sec:collection}
23	
24	Per-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.
25	
26	\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}
27	For a task instance $t$ and modality $m \in \mathcal{M}$, a \emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:
28	\begin{equation}
29	    \tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \ldots, o_T, a_T)
30	\end{equation}
31	where $T \leq T_{\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), ensuring that performance differences arise solely from the modality.
32	
33	Each trajectory $\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\tau) \in \mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\tau) \in \{0, 1\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:
34	\begin{multline}
35	    \bigl(s^{\tt int}, s^{\tt det}, d^{\tt int}, d^{\tt det}\bigr) \triangleq \\
36	    \bigl(s(\tau_{\tt int}),\, s(\tau_{\tt det}),\, d(\tau_{\tt int}),\, d(\tau_{\tt det})\bigr)
37	\end{multline}
38	
39	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
40	
41	\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}
42	\label{sec:labeling}
43	
44	We define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:
45	\begin{multline}
46	    L\!\bigl(\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}, \tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}\bigr) \;\rightarrow \\
47	    \ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref},\; {\tt int\text{-}pref},\; {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}
48	\end{multline}
49	
50	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, reflecting the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.
51	
52	\begin{figure}[t]
53	\centering
54	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
55	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
56	\label{fig:pipeline}
57	\end{figure}
58	
59	\begin{algorithm}[t]
60	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
61	\label{alg:labeling}
62	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
63	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
64	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
65	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
66	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
67	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
68	\ENDFOR
69	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
70	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
71	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
72	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
73	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
74	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
75	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
76	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
77	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
78	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
79	\ELSE
80	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
81	\ENDIF
82	\RETURN $\ell$
83	\end{algorithmic}
84	\end{algorithm}
85	
86	The four mutually exclusive cases correspond to:
87	\begin{enumerate}
88	    \item \textbf{Death asymmetry} (lines 5--8): $d^{\tt int} \neq d^{\tt det}$ $\rightarrow$ the surviving strategy is preferred. This accounts for 78.9\% of all non-tie labels (75 of 95).
89	    \item \textbf{Both dead} (lines 9--10): $d^{\tt int} = d^{\tt det} = 1$ $\rightarrow$ ${\tt no\_preference}$.
90	    \item \textbf{Score comparison} (lines 11--14): both survive and $s^{\tt det} \neq s^{\tt int}$ $\rightarrow$ the higher-scoring strategy is preferred.
91	    \item \textbf{Tied alive} (lines 15--16): both survive and $s^{\tt det} = s^{\tt int}$ $\rightarrow$ ${\tt no\_preference}$.
92	\end{enumerate}
93	
94	Label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
95	
96	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
97	\label{sec:daf}
98	
99	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:
100	\begin{equation}
101	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
102	\end{equation}
103	The approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
104	
105	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
106	\begin{equation}
107	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
108	\end{equation}%
109	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1, indicating death avoidance overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
110	
111	DAF is well-defined when $\Delta_{\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. DAF should therefore be interpreted relative to the environment's penalty magnitude: it quantifies the penalty's amplification of death asymmetry, not a universal constant. We report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\%) as complementary metrics.
112	
113	To assess sensitivity to penalty magnitude, we recalculate DAF under hypothetical death penalties (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}): $P{=}10 \rightarrow$ DAF $= 50.4\%$, $P{=}50 \rightarrow 82.6\%$, $P{=}100 \rightarrow 90.4\%$, $P{=}200 \rightarrow 95.0\%$. Death avoidance remains the dominant component across all penalty magnitudes, though its relative contribution scales with penalty severity. As a penalty-independent companion metric, 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\S\ref{sec:labeling}); death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.
114	
115	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
116	We quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$ resamples). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$, and DAF, and derives the percentile-based 95\% CI. The aggregate DAF CI is $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$, excluding both 50\% and 100\%. Per-task bootstrap CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 task types; \texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, $\Delta = 21.8$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size rather than absence of signal.
117	
118	We resample at the episode level rather than the step level because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison: each episode pair yields one label, and steps within an episode are sequentially dependent. Episode-level resampling preserves this correlation structure and matches the granularity at which DAF is defined.
119	
120	\subsection{Signal Concentration Index}
121	\label{sec:sci}
122	
123	DAF quantifies \emph{how much} routing signal derives from death avoidance; a complementary question is whether this signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We define the Signal Concentration Index (SCI) using the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions.
124	
125	Let $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:
126	\begin{equation}
127	    c_k = \frac{n_k \cdot \delta_k}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \cdot \delta_j}
128	\end{equation}
129	where $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\delta_k = d_k^{\tt int} - d_k^{\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\}$:
130	\begin{equation}
131	    \text{SCI} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^K \sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \sum_{k=1}^K c_k}
132	\end{equation}
133	
134	SCI $\in [0, 1]$: a value near 0 indicates uniform distribution of routing signal across task types (systematic phenomenon); a value near 1 indicates concentration in a single task type (isolated phenomenon). In our data, SCI $= 0.2147$, indicating low concentration. The top-1 task type contributes 14.3\% of total signal, top-2 contributes 27.5\%, and top-3 contributes 39.8\%; no single task dominates. Of 11 task types, 10 exhibit nonzero death signal (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths in both modalities). Together with DAF, SCI establishes that death avoidance is both the dominant routing signal (DAF $= 90.4\%$) and a systematic phenomenon distributed across nearly all task types (SCI $= 0.2147$).
135
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\\S\\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning (deep internal) against direct-answer generation (fast internal) on 6 overlapping task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition) found no significant difference on any task (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes in this setting.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), ensuring that performance differences arise solely from the modality.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, reflecting the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four mutually exclusive cases correspond to:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Death asymmetry} (lines 5--8): $d^{\\tt int} \\neq d^{\\tt det}$ $\\rightarrow$ the surviving strategy is preferred. This accounts for 78.9\\% of all non-tie labels (75 of 95).\n    \\item \\textbf{Both dead} (lines 9--10): $d^{\\tt int} = d^{\\tt det} = 1$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n    \\item \\textbf{Score comparison} (lines 11--14): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} \\neq s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ the higher-scoring strategy is preferred.\n    \\item \\textbf{Tied alive} (lines 15--16): both survive and $s^{\\tt det} = s^{\\tt int}$ $\\rightarrow$ ${\\tt no\\_preference}$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nLabel distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1, indicating death avoidance overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF is well-defined when $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} > 0$; in our data, the minimum per-task $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is $+9.58$ (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}), ensuring numerical stability. Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. DAF should therefore be interpreted relative to the environment's penalty magnitude: it quantifies the penalty's amplification of death asymmetry, not a universal constant. We report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\\%) as complementary metrics.\n\nTo assess sensitivity to penalty magnitude, we recalculate DAF under hypothetical death penalties (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}): $P{=}10 \\rightarrow$ DAF $= 50.4\\%$, $P{=}50 \\rightarrow 82.6\\%$, $P{=}100 \\rightarrow 90.4\\%$, $P{=}200 \\rightarrow 95.0\\%$. As a penalty-independent companion metric, 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels (75 of 95) are driven by death asymmetry rather than score comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:labeling}); death avoidance dominates routing signal regardless of penalty magnitude.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$ resamples). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, and DAF, and derives the percentile-based 95\\% CI. 33	\section*{Limitations}
```
```text
34	
35	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:discussion}, Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.
36	
37	\section*{Ethics Statement}
38	
39	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
40	
41	\bibliography{references}
42	
43	\appendix
44	
45	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
46	\label{app:labeling}
47	
48	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
49	
50	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
51	\label{app:per-task}
52	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful: task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}

82	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
83	The 11 included task types are those exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening, not those with the highest death rates. The deterministic-strategy death rate is comparable between included (17.8\%) and excluded (14.9\%) task types; prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks. Rather, the key distinction is the \emph{presence} of routing signal: all 11 included tasks have $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \neq 0$ (non-zero performance gap on surviving episodes), while 53\% of excluded tasks show $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ (neither strategy outperforms the other even when both survive, typically because both time out before making progress). \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: it has zero deaths under both strategies yet is included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
84	
85	\section{Correlation Robustness}
86	\label{app:correlation}
87	
88	The Pearson $r = 0.9698$ reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp} is robust across multiple diagnostics. Spearman rank correlation is $\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \times 10^{-8}$); the relationship is not driven by outliers or non-linearity. A nonparametric bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) yields 95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$.

114	\section{Router Training Details}
115	\label{app:router-details}
116	
117	We use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.
118	
119	With gradient accumulation of 1 (effective batch size 4), routing performance is highly sensitive to the evaluation split; this suggests overfitting to specific train/validation partition boundaries. With gradient accumulation of 8 (effective batch size 32), step-level performance stabilizes across splits (${\sim}54\%$ balanced accuracy). All reported results use gradient accumulation 8.
120	
121	Under step-level evaluation with a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\% to 58.1\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, reflecting threshold sensitivity. However, episode-level evaluation reveals that this apparent discrimination does not generalize: balanced accuracy drops to $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ across 8 cross-split seeds (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.
122	
123	\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. We evaluate at two granularities: \emph{step-level} (steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets) and \emph{episode-level} (entire episodes held out). Step-level accuracy ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) exceeds chance, indicating the router captures some signal, but episode-level accuracy ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below chance) shows this does not generalize to unseen episodes. Augmentation-based data scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}) provides no improvement; real-data scaling remains open. Always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal given DAF $= 90.4\%$. The main text (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reports controlled 4-seed results at three scales; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) provides further detail.
124	
125	\begin{table}[h]
126	\centering
127	\small
128	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
129	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
130	\toprule
131	Hyperparameter & Classification Head & Implicit Router \\
132	\midrule
133	Base model & Qwen3-1.7B & Qwen3-1.7B \\
134	Architecture & Linear + LoRA & SFT + LoRA \\
135	LoRA $r$ / $\alpha$ & 16 / 32 & 16 / 32 \\
136	Learning rate & 2e-5 & 2e-4 \\
137	Optimizer & AdamW & AdamW \\
138	Epochs & 3 & 3 \\
139	Batch size (per device) & 4 & 4 \\
140	Gradient accumulation & 8 & 8 \\
141	Warmup ratio & 0.1 & 0.1 \\
142	Weight decay & 0.01 & 0.01 \\
143	Max sequence length & 512 & 512 \\
144	Seeds & \{42\} & \{42, 123, 456, 789\} \\
145	\bottomrule
146	\end{tabular}}
147	\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Both architectures use the same base model with LoRA adaptation. The classification head degenerates across all configurations; the implicit router results are reported as 4-seed mean$\pm$std.}
148	\label{tab:hyperparams}
149	\end{table}
150	
151	\section{Seed Robustness}
152	\label{app:seed-robustness}
153	
154	We assess router generalization at two evaluation granularities: \emph{step-level}, where steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets, and \emph{episode-level}, where entire episodes are held out. Both use cross-split evaluation (seeds $\{42, \ldots, 49\}$, 8 seeds total), where the data partition and training seed both vary. Table~\ref{tab:seed-robustness} reports per-seed results.
155	
156	\begin{table}[h]
157	\centering
158	\small
159	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
160	\toprule
161	Eval.\ Granularity & Seed & Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
162	\midrule
163	Step-level & 42 & 57.06 \\
164	(same-episode & 43 & 63.99 \\
165	leakage) & 44 & 55.43 \\
166	 & 45 & 56.14 \\
167	 & 46 & 55.45 \\
168	 & 47 & 50.08 \\
169	 & 48 & 48.03 \\
170	 & 49 & 59.30 \\
171	\cmidrule{2-3}
172	 & Mean$\pm$std & $54.1 \pm 5.4$ \\
173	\midrule
174	Episode-level & 42 & 48.1 \\
175	(held-out & 43 & 47.9 \\
176	episodes) & 44 & 49.6 \\
177	 & 45 & 49.4 \\
178	 & 46 & 48.5 \\
179	 & 47 & 43.1 \\
180	 & 48 & 45.4 \\
181	 & 49 & 44.8 \\
182	\cmidrule{2-3}
183	 & Mean$\pm$std & $47.1 \pm 2.2$ \\
184	\midrule
185	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{Always-det (= majority-vote)} & 50.0 \\
186	\bottomrule
187	\end{tabular}
188	\caption{Per-seed router performance under cross-split evaluation (8 seeds, generation-based, \texttt{enable\_thinking=False}). Step-level evaluation allows steps from the same episode in both train and validation sets (same-episode leakage). Episode-level evaluation holds out entire episodes; the resulting $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ is below the 50\% chance baseline. A data scaling ablation (Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling}) confirms this is structural rather than data-limited.}
189	\label{tab:seed-robustness}
190	\end{table}
191	
192	The contrast between step-level ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) and episode-level ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$) evaluation reveals a generalization gap: the step-level signal likely reflects memorization of episode-specific context (enabled by same-episode leakage) rather than extraction of generalizable routing features. At episode level, the router performs below chance across all 8 seeds (range: 43.1\%--49.6\%). Augmentation-based data scaling (Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling}) from 25\% to 800\% ($32{\times}$ range) provides no improvement, converging to the always-deterministic baseline ($50\%$). However, paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class without generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling remains an open question. With DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), always-deterministic trivially solves the episode-level routing problem, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router.
193	
194	\paragraph{Data scaling ablation.} To test whether the episode-level generalization failure stems from data scarcity, we vary training set size from 25\% to 800\% of the original via paraphrase-based data augmentation (synonym substitution + template variation on step observations), holding the episode-level validation set fixed. Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling} reports the results.
195	
196	\begin{table}[h]
197	\centering
198	\small
199	\begin{tabular}{@{}rccc@{}}
200	\toprule
201	Scale & Samples & Det\% & Ep.\ Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
202	\midrule
203	25\% & 433 & ${\sim}90$ & $49.5 \pm 0.3$ \\
204	50\% & 867 & ${\sim}90$ & $51.5$ \\
205	100\% & 1{,}735 & 89.6 & $49.9 \pm 0.1$ \\
206	200\% & 3{,}470 & 94.2 & $50.0 \pm 0.0$ \\
207	800\% & 13{,}880 & ${\sim}97.7$ & $51.0 \pm 1.1$ \\
208	\midrule
209	Always-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\
210	\bottomrule
211	\end{tabular}
212	\caption{Data scaling ablation: episode-level balanced accuracy across training set sizes (25\%--800\% of original, $32{\times}$ range). Internal recall $= 0.0$ at all scales (complete majority-class collapse). Det\%: fraction of deterministic-preferred labels in training data. The 50\% scale is a single seed; all others report mean$\pm$std across seeds.}
213	\label{tab:data-scaling}
214	\end{table}
215	
216	All scales hover at ${\sim}50\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy (equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline), with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics, a $5.5{\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.
217	
218	Augmentation disproportionately increases the majority (deterministic) class: Det\% rises from 89.6\% at 100\% to 94.2\% at 200\% and ${\sim}97.7\%$ at 800\%. However, the complete collapse to majority-class prediction (internal recall $= 0.0$) occurs even at the smallest unaugmented scale (25\%, ${\sim}90\%$ det); this is not an augmentation artifact. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.
219	
220	Decomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\% \pm 4.2\%$ (95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \pm 0.011$ (95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.
221	
222	\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined; the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\% to 73.0\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \emph{is} the task type, i.e., whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.
223	
224	\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}
225	\label{app:penalty}
226	
227	\begin{table}[h]
228	\centering
229	\small
230	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
231	\toprule
232	$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\% CI \\
233	\midrule
234	0 & 7.5\% & [$-36.7$, $83.1$] \\
235	10 & 50.4\% & [$19.8$, $94.5$] \\
236	25 & 70.8\% & [$48.5$, $97.1$] \\
237	50 & 82.6\% & [$67.6$, $98.4$] \\
238	100\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\% & [$81.5$, $99.1$] \\
239	200 & 95.0\% & [$90.0$, $99.5$] \\
240	500 & 97.9\% & [$95.8$, $99.8$] \\
241	\bottomrule
242	\end{tabular}
243	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Bootstrap 95\% CIs from 1{,}000 resamples. \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
244	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
245	\end{table}
246	
247	The non-death score gap $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.
248	
249	\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}
250	The $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.
251	
252	\begin{table}[h]
253	\centering
254	\small
255	\begin{tabular}{@{}rccl@{}}
256	\toprule
257	$P$ & $r$ & $p$ & Sig.\ ($\alpha{=}0.05$) \\
258	\midrule
259	10 & $-0.08$ & $0.813$ & No \\
260	25 & $0.51$ & $0.108$ & No \\
261	50 & $0.86$ & $0.0006$ & Yes \\
262	100\textsuperscript{*} & $0.97$ & $8 \times 10^{-7}$ & Yes \\
263	200 & $0.99$ & $1 \times 10^{-9}$ & Yes \\
264	\bottomrule
265	\end{tabular}
266	\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \geq 50$, reflecting the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}
267	\label{tab:penalty-correlation}
268	\end{table}
269	
270	This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal; it is effectively the \emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ reduces to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\Delta_{\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\text{DAF} = \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.
271	
272	\section{Cross-Model Verification}
273	\label{app:cross-model}
274	
275	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.6\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
276	
277	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
278	\label{app:failure-modes}
279	
280	The most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\% of episodes, primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.
281	
282	\section{Ablation Studies}
283	\label{app:ablations}
284	
285	\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\ref{tab:capacity}).
286	
287	\begin{table}[h]
288	\centering
289	\small
290	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
291	\toprule
292	Model & Step Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) & Ep.\ Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
293	\midrule
294	Qwen3-0.6B & $56.4 \pm 3.5$ & $50.7 \pm 2.6$ \\
295	Qwen3-1.7B & $58.2 \pm 4.0$ & $43.1 \pm 1.0$ \\
296	Qwen3-4B & $59.2 \pm 3.5$ & $\mathbf{59.0 \pm 1.9}$ \\
297	\midrule
298	Always-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\
299	\bottomrule
300	\end{tabular}
301	\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy (4 seeds, identical configuration across all scales). Step-level accuracy is flat ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0\%$, $p < 0.005$).}
302	\label{tab:capacity}
303	\end{table}
304	
305	Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance), indicating step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The dominant explanation is that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.
306	
307	\paragraph{4B end-to-end deployment.} We deploy the 4B router in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}275$ episodes)\footnote{The 4B evaluation uses $N{=}275$ episodes (vs.\ $N{=}245$ for baselines in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) due to expanded task coverage during data collection. Score distributions are comparable across the shared task types.} to test whether improved episode-level accuracy translates to deployment gains. The 4B router scores $-10.72$ (95\% CI: $[-15.69, -5.52]$), nearly identical to the 1.7B router ($-10.70$) and not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). Step-level accuracy is comparable across scales ($56$--$59\%$) yet deployment score remains flat at ${\sim}{-10.7}$. Routing accuracy does not translate to deployment gains when death avoidance dominates the signal. The analytical framework (DAF decomposition) identifies this ceiling without requiring expensive router training at multiple scales.
308	
309	\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.
310	
311	\section{Cross-Environment Analysis: ALFWorld}
312	\label{app:cross-env}
313	
314	We evaluate on ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment with 6 task types (json\_2.1.1 split, valid\_seen, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric), using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty; failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step (an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch).
315	
316	The success rate gap is large: 27.4\% (deterministic) vs.\ 3.0\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution (Table~\ref{tab:cross-env}) shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance.

323	\end{figure}
```
